ParlaMint-DK_20170420101922	Dementia is a terrible disease which already affects many thousands of Danes today, and it is expected that it will affect even more in the coming years. It was therefore also gratifying that the Danish People's Party, together with the government and the gamble pool parties, agreed to allocate 470 million. kr. for the period 2016-2019 for initiatives in the National Dementia Action Plan. However, that is not good enough. Money was put off in health care for investigation, treatment, family work and networks, but many of the other challenges relating to the demented were not addressed. The reason for this query is the increasing problem with younger people with dementia being sent in work testing, job training and resource development. The problem is two-sided. Firstly, it slows down the granting of early retirement pensions, which is a huge problem for many, as they have often gone out of the labour market before investigation and diagnosis and are therefore financially under pressure. Secondly, the dementia patient is at risk of becoming even more ill, as the stress that it is to try hard with a job actually affects the course of illness in a negative way. We do not think that is right, and that is why we hope that the Minister will ensure that people affected by dementia do not face additional challenges because of their illness. I look forward to the debate. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420102109	Thank you. Then it is the Minister of Employment for a reply.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420102120	Thank you. And thank you for the motivation from the Danish People's Party in relation to the question that we have here today in Parliament. After all, it is a question debate about people who are diagnosed with dementia and about the efforts that stem from the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs. The Danish People's Party wants answers to how I, as a minister, want to ensure that people with dementia do not have to go through job clearance courses, resource processes or other offers. Let me start by saying that it is my clear conviction that it is not certain diagnoses that should dictate what should be put into effect and what should not be put into effect. I believe that we should always be based on the individual citizen. This is the foundation stone and the basic essence of the whole employment effort. So what I want to ensure is that no one, regardless of diagnosis, will have to go through resource sequences if it does not help to develop their ability to work. So we must not set up internships that do not develop people, but instead are seen as degrading and meaningless. On the other hand, I do not think it would be sensible to write off everyone with a dementia diagnosis, for example. the possibility of a flex job. But let me start somewhere else, because first of all I would like to take this opportunity to say that the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs has basically been and is right. The conciliation circle also agrees on this. The reform supports the support of as many people as possible to achieve or maintain links with the labour market, and we can also see that very fortunately works as intended. But let me also say quite clearly today that it is no secret that there have been examples of flows of resources that have not been of benefit to the citizens. That is why I am glad that this issue is being addressed. This is a good opportunity to clarify what the purpose of resource development is and also to say what it is that we have set in motion to ensure it. After all, as most people know, the circle behind the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs has just reached agreement on a new package of initiatives which strengthens the municipalities' work on resource development. The initiative package includes, among other things: a new letter of orientation stating that active action must help citizens closer to the labour market and that, of course, action must never exacerbate the health of citizens. It has, of course, been a major theme in the few months I have been Minister for Employment in up to several consultations where I have received that question from both Enhedslisten's Mr. Finn Sørensen, but also from the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party, Mr. Bent Bøgsted. We are also being made clear that the municipalities should not set up business practices with the aim of documenting what the citizen does not document does not mean that everything has to be tested in practice. Pre-retirement pensions are granted on the basis of a health assessment of the overall situation of citizens, including medical evidence in the case. I very much hope that these clarifications will help to clarify this issue, because it is, after all, absolutely the alpha and the omega that the efforts made in the process of resource development make sense and thus also develop the citizen. If it is pointless to take active action, the citizen must, of course, have an early retirement pension. I would also like to point out that last year the conciliation circle amended the legislation so that municipalities can grant early retirement to citizens more quickly and more easily, where it is obvious that there is no labour market perspective. The rehabilitation team should no longer be involved in these cases. This is precisely what we did to ensure that you do not have to wait longer than is necessary to get your early retirement when there is no other option. At the same time, I would also like to stress today that it is absolutely essential that we have an employment system that makes sense and in which it is always the needs and opportunities of the individual citizen that form the basis for what is being put into effect. That is why I do not believe that it is a viable way of allowing certain diagnoses to dictate what should be done and what should not be done. As I have mentioned a few times, I think that the effort should always be based on the individual citizen and therefore also on the needs of the individual citizen. Two people with the same diagnosis can have very different processes and challenges, and this also applies to dementia. Some citizens are diagnosed with dementia at an early stage, so that drugs can be used which can help delay development and where there can still be potential in a labour market perspective. It may perhaps make sense here to engage in an employment-oriented approach which, of course, must be organised in the light of the individual's wishes and needs and resources. It can, for example, be done. be a flex job. I also think that the question gives rise to a reminder that a broad majority in the Folketing has basically strengthened and created a much better interdisciplinary effort for vulnerable citizens. We have done so because we want as many people as possible to become part of the working community. All municipalities now have a multi-disciplinary rehabilitation team dealing with cases where there is a need for more than just an employment-oriented effort. Clinical functions have also been established to ensure that municipalities can obtain qualified health professional advice and guidance in the more complex cases. In addition, there has been much better opportunity to create flex jobs for people who have more extensive spare needs. It has also become possible to continue in flexible jobs even if the ability to work gradually deteriorates. All in all, therefore, there have been better opportunities to provide the necessary support for people who are facing major challenges, such as: difficult illness, which makes it impossible for them to have a working life after all. From this point of view too, I think it makes sense that it is the individual's specific situation and ability to work that constitute the compass of what needs to be put into effect and not necessarily a particular diagnosis. However, I would also like to reiterate and make it clear that it is, of course, a precondition that active action can help the individual citizen closer to the labour market. If, on the other hand, the ability to work is permanently and substantially reduced, an early retirement must of course be granted. Finally, let me stress that the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs must be evaluated in the course of 2017. The whole reform must be highlighted from all the leaders and edges, and we will look at resource developments in particular carefully. There is no doubt that resource development has the full attention of the entire conciliation circle, because the effort of resource development must make sense. If the evaluation shows that there are still too many examples of the failure to make any sense, then of course we will also have a political debate on it in the conciliation circle. And, of course, I also invite all the parties of the Folketing to take part in the evaluation which will now take place here during 2017. I am also quite convinced that we will have ample opportunity to discuss this in the Employment Committee, perhaps also in the Parliamentary Committee on Legal Affairs and, I hope, also here in the Parliamentary Chamber.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420102929	Thank you very much and thank you very much to the Minister for your reply. But I think it is a little easy for a government to go out and look out for a national action plan for people with dementia, and then you will not do anything for those people, because it is a rather special disease. I think it is very easy to say that it is individual, and if it is discovered at an early stage, you can get medicine yes, the average life expectancy is approx. Seven years from the time the disease is discovered. When the speech usually falls on people with dementia, we often think of elderly people who have retired and where the biggest challenge is care. The reality is that more and more younger people are being affected by dementia. At least 1,500 people under the age of 65 have been diagnosed with dementia. However, there must be reservations about dark figures, as not all younger people have been diagnosed with dementia. It takes a very long time to get it, so you are actually really sick when you get the diagnosis. In the Danish People's Party we are also very pleased with the national action plan. We are just wondering why we are not looking at all the other challenges that are in other ministries. What is the use of rapid investigation, better treatment and increased competence and family work if the person with dementia is to be dragged through a system just for the sake of the system? I discovered the challenge in this area when I saw a DR documentary broadcast. The main character tells us that he is in resource sequences and that he cannot concentrate at all, because his thoughts are on what should happen to his family. They were to move to a place where he could live, where he could live with his illness with his family. He became more and more stressed and actually became more and more ill. There was also a story about a woman with dementia who was in a wheelchair and who had time to die before she got her early retirement. These are the stories we hear every day. I contacted the Alzheimer's Association to ask if this is a common problem. I'm afraid it is. The Alzheimer's Association is experiencing an increasing problem of sending younger people with dementia into work testing, job training and resource development by their municipality. And as I said at the outset, it is a two-sided problem. Firstly, the granting of early retirement is delayed, which is a huge problem for many people, as they are getting out of the labour market because of diagnosis, and some believe that it is stress and so on. Secondly, every time they are in the work test, it is a failure and they are becoming increasingly ill by it. You will also receive more inquiries on the Dementia line about problems than you have in the past. They receive inquiries from worried dementia consultants in the municipalities and from professionals in the investigation units. I spoke to a family member yesterday I asked the Alzheimer's Association if they knew some people with these diseases, and they actually knew a lot of people who could tell what it looks like for many of the younger people affected by dementia. Her friend's story is a typical example of what it looks like. A 52-year-old woman gets hit and gets diagnosed with dementia. Fortunately, her friend is a social worker and therefore knows the rules. So not everyone is as lucky. After an extension of her sickness benefit by 26 weeks extra, she received a standard letter that she should find out how she could get back into the labour market, knowing that she had diagnosed dementia and an average life span of 7 years back. It has to be said here that he himself was very unrealistic about her ability to continue working as a teacher as well. After she lost her first job, she got a flex job in a forest nursery where she knew the children. That must be fatal. Because of her good social worker friend, she was awarded a pension, and thus she was also able to receive her own pension, which she has saved up for through her working life. You can't figure that out either. At the age of 52 you have to go through a lot of resource development and clarification when you have an average of seven years to live, and then you cannot get paid the pension that you have earned and saved up for you at least not. This social worker who works in Hvidovre Municipality could tell about a handful of people sitting in just Hvidovre Municipality who have gone from house to home with the diagnosis of dementia. We simply cannot be familiar with this. Then we can look up that we can make a good national action plan for demente, but what the hell will it do if we do not help where it hurts? That is why, of course, we in the Danish People's Party want you to be able to obtain an early retirement pension when you have a dementia diagnosis if that is what you need. Of course, we must also ensure that, if you want to remain in the labour market, you can get help with this. But today it's just not the way you do it. It may be that the minister thinks and says that several ministers have said that they are not going to work if they are too sick, but then do something about the municipalities that keep doing it. We have a proposal for adoption supported by Dansk Folkeparti, Enhedslisten and Alternativet: Suggestions for adoption The Danish Parliament requires the government to ensure that people with diagnosis dementia should not be sent in job clearance or resource progressions against their will. The Folketing also wants people with dementia who want to remain in the labour market to have better opportunities for it. V 78).

Of course, that's not okay. The whole idea of the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs was to look at the individual, to look at what each person has of problems, but also of opportunities. If there is no possibility of recovery or of getting closer to the labour market, it is clear that it is an early retirement that is needed, but if there is an opportunity to get closer to the labour market or, possibly, through a flexible job, to get into a working community, one must try to do so. I would also like to make it quite clear that the purpose of the reform was not and still is not that people should get sick from being in resource or job clearance. It must be seen as a good tool for helping the individual, it must develop them, and it must seem pointless, no, it shouldn't When that's said, it would be stupid of me to think that this works very well, because it doesn't, and if it did, we wouldn't have stood here, and then we wouldn't have had all the debates that we've had over time. There is a reason why the Danish People's Party is taking this up, and for a reason why many of these stories are actually running in the press, because there are still municipalities that do not treat these people as intended by the law and that do not do it the right way. Dementia is a terrible disease, but you can also ask yourself whether dementia should then be written into the law, whether there should be something special for dementia. I believe that the whole idea of the labour market reforms that we have made is that we should look at the individual's potential for development or lack of it and assess what the further process should be. It should not be assessed on the basis of a diagnosis, but together with the individual, it should be assessed whether there is a possibility of a positive development and I said that together with the individual person should feel that they are part of the process, the individual should feel that they are part of their future, so that they should not feel insecure and hunted. This is incredibly important to us and I have no doubt for a second that it is extremely important for all the conciliation parties. Therefore, on behalf of the Social Democratic Party, the Left, the Liberal Alliance, the Radical Left, the Socialist People's Party and the Conservatives, I would like to make the following points: Proposals for adoption The Danish Parliament notes that: job clearance processes, resource processes or other offers must be implemented with the aim of helping the citizen closer to the labour market. The action must never worsen the health of the citizen; it is the individual's specific situation that is the starting point for what offers and benefits are relevant, citizens with long-term and significantly reduced working capacity, where it is evident that it is pointless to take action to achieve a link with the labour market, are entitled to early retirement, regardless of the diagnosis involved, and that the conciliation circle behind the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs has agreed on a new package of initiatives that strengthens the municipalities' work on resource development. The Danish Parliament also notes that the reform of early retirement and flexible jobs will have to be evaluated during 2017. V 79). Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420131933	Thank you. And then the question is answered by the Minister for Children and Social Affairs, who is given the floor here. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420131945	Thank you, President. The question debate we have now reached is about a better and more coherent procedure for people with dementia. Specifically, I have been asked how I will ensure that people with dementia can have a better and more coherent casework with as few case workers as possible and how I will ensure that applications for social benefits, help or support are made easier to access and fill. In fact, I am very, very pleased that we have this question debate, because it helps to highlight the fact that citizens with dementia can have diverse challenges that require a coherent and holistic process in the municipalities. Before answering the question, I would like to start by saying more generally that we in the government have a special focus on citizens with dementia and their relatives. And as Mr. Jeppe Jakobsen just before, we concluded an agreement shortly before Christmas with the pool parties on the National Dementia Action Plan 2025, with a total of 470 million. kr. will be given priority to 23 new initiatives designed to give a significant boost in this area. And this is the first time that so much money has been allocated to that area. The Dementia Action Plan will include: help to create coherence and quality of action towards citizens with dementia and their relatives and to support Denmark becoming a more dementia-friendly country. One of the initiatives means that 12-15 centres of advice and activity are being set up throughout the country, and at these centres a number of offers are being made to citizens with dementia and their relatives, so that it will be possible for the same centre to get advice on a number of the different issues and challenges that one encounters when the disease hits. If I am then going to reply specifically to the question, I would say that the administrative rules say that it is the municipalities that are responsible for organising the procedure. The municipalities also have a separate obligation to ensure that citizens who may find it difficult to understand and, above all, difficult to understand procedures are also given the opportunity to participate in the handling of their cases. In the government, we believe that responsibility for the organisation of the procedure must be maintained in the municipalities. We do so because we believe that the municipalities are the closest to the citizens and also have the best conditions for organising the case-file on the basis of individual considerations in individual cases. For example: it will be possible for the municipality to organise a tailor-made procedure for a citizen with dementia who may need a little extra time to explain a procedural step or get help in filling out an application form. It may also be that, as a citizen with dementia, you need a little more time to guide choices in terms of help and treatment, and that is as well in relation to public and private, so that free choice is also strengthened. Here, too, dementia coordinators can play a crucial role by, for example, providing information and reference to the different local authorities' offers in the area of dementia. I am therefore also very pleased that according to KL today all municipalities have employed dementia coordinators. Indeed, in many places they are responsible for coordinating qualified action for citizens with dementia and, above all, for relatives. However, even if responsibility for the organisation of the procedure is located in the municipalities, this does not mean that nothing is being done centrally to support that the municipalities make the case as simple and understandable as possible and not least as transparent as possible for the individual citizen. So there are continuously developed tools that the municipalities can use. These are tools that can be used in the cross-sectoral organisation and coordination as well as within the individual municipality. The tools are general for the social area, but they can of course also be used in relation to dementia. The tools should help to ensure a better and more coherent procedure with as few case officers as possible. As appropriate tools here, I can mention coordinators in the adult field and the ODA project. In relation to adult coordinators, there is an action guide for the adult coordination function, where the action guide contains tools and associated implementation guides, which are central to cases where there are many actors involved and where the citizen also has continuous contact with the municipality. There are also tools and complementary guides that can be used in relation to new citizens with complex cases and to citizens who cannot coordinate a course themselves. The ODA project aims to improve the efforts of citizens with dementia and their relatives. The ODA contains tools that can help ensure a more coordinated interaction between the municipal authority, providers of care for the elderly and voluntary social organisations. And there are also ODA tools for analysing and planning the municipal organisation and coordination of efforts. So, in addition to the tools to support the municipalities' organisation of procedures in a more simple, understandable and transparent way, we in the government are also in the process of implementing regulatory changes to facilitate procedures in the field of aid for the benefit and benefit of both citizens and municipalities. This is done in the context of the review of the adult provision of the Services Act, where we are currently making the changes to the rules which will implement the political agreement of November last year. One of the regulatory changes is that we create the legal basis for the municipalities to be able to use a simplified procedure for processing applications for AT and consumer goods. And the procedure, which can be used in simple and unambiguous cases, means that decisions will be taken on the basis of the application alone, so that a remedy or consumer goods can be obtained more easily, but also more quickly. It could, for example, be done. be a GPS for a citizen with dementia. Another rule change is to make it compulsory for municipalities to use declarations of faith and law when regranting aids. This means that in cases where the reduction in function is unchanged and there is simply a need for a replacement of an aid, there is no need for a new procedure with the re-filling of application forms, a new collection of information, etc. etc. So it is enough for the citizen to sign a Declaration of Faith and Law as proof that the need is still present. I very much hope that these regulatory changes will help to facilitate procedures for both municipalities and citizens, and of course also for citizens with dementia. If I am finally to pick up a little, then it is the government's view that the responsibility for organising the procedure is best served by being kept out in the municipalities, but from a central point of view we must of course support that the municipalities make the case as simple, understandable, clear and transparent as possible for the individual citizen. For this reason, tools are also constantly being developed to help ensure better and more coherent procedures, as well as facilitating procedures in specific areas. However, in addition to this, the government will also discuss with KL how to increase the general awareness of municipalities to ensure better and simpler and more coherent procedures.

One of the unreasonable things that is happening now is that people who fear having a dementia disease may be in danger of waiting up to 50 weeks to get through a trial at all and to be solved. This happens despite legal requirements for rapid investigation, which can be read on the Alzheimer's Association's website. This is, of course, quite unreasonable, because the better and the faster the processing and investigation is, the faster you get clarification and the faster you can get a meaningful everyday life with a better quality of life. The new national action plan for dementia, which has been mentioned sometimes today and in which we are also part of, provides for 145 million. kr. for better and faster detection of dementia. At the same time, we must also ensure that the government, together with the municipalities, pays attention to ensuring simpler and more coherent procedures, as is also mentioned in the adoption text in which we are involved. And we hope that these things together can make the everyday and life situation of the individual with dementia better.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420141626	Thank you to the rapporteur for the questions. Then we are ready to answer the question, and that is the Minister of Justice. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420141641	Thank you. Thank you for the question, in which I am asked as Minister of Justice to explain how I will ensure that persons who, for example, have the diagnosis of dementia are not to be dragged through legal proceedings and be ordered to pay the costs of the proceedings, knowing in advance that they cannot be judged on the grounds of permanent insanity. First of all, I would like to make it clear that I fully recognise and share the concern about the permanent involvement of the judicial system in criminal proceedings raised by the questioners. In other words, I have a great deal of sympathy with the difficult situation in which people who are permanently incompetent find themselves. It is my basic view that we as a society are obliged to deal with vulnerable groups of people, and this also applies, of course, to the long-term intolerable or mentally deviant, as they are also called, even though it may not sound so good, but that is the term, but do not misunderstand me now. The questioners and I are in complete agreement on that. Before I go into what I think of the question, I would like to outline how our penal system is actually designed in this area. Criminal proceedings against mentally deviant persons are dealt with from a different point of view from normal criminal proceedings, and that is with good reason. Mental deviants often find it difficult, and they do not always understand the general standards of society or the consequences of their actions. In such cases, therefore, the prosecution may, in certain cases, give the person an indictment instead of bringing the case before the court for an order for a measure to be imposed. Indictment is used, especially when you have already received a treatment sentence. If, as a result of past crime, you are already subject to a measure, the case may be closed with an indictment, unless there are very special circumstances which argue against it. The prosecution is therefore already fully focused on these cases and can end with indictment rather than initiate criminal proceedings in the courts. It is therefore by no means all cases of this type that end up as criminal proceedings in the courts, and I think that is an important point to make. It also shows that there is already an understanding in the legal system of the difficult situation, such as: permanent inhabile can be found in. The cases that then end up in the courts will typically be more serious cases where there may also be consideration for the victim or for the sense of justice. If a person is found guilty of criminal proceedings but is punishable by his or her psychic condition, the courts have the possibility of judging a court judgment if it is appropriate to prevent further offences. This means that the mentally deviant perpetrator, for example. will be able to stay in a settlement or may be subject to outpatient treatment. It is an important point here that although the person concerned is not usually punished by, for example, a prison sentence is imposed on a so-called measure when further offences need to be prevented. I think this is the right arrangement, because we cannot have a community of law where certain groups of people can commit without responsibility, for example. violence, without society reacting and trying to prevent it from happening again. On the other hand, of course, we need a legal system that takes account of the very special situation of these people, both in assessing whether criminal proceedings should be conducted at all and in assessing how criminal proceedings should be decided. In this respect, let me make it quite clear that, fortunately, this is also how our legal system works today. There is an appropriate balance between understanding and inclusiveness in relation to people who face these very specific challenges and the concern for victims and the need to prevent further crime. Another question, then, is whether it is reasonable that, if one is found guilty of criminal proceedings but is free from punishment as a result of his psychic condition, one should in all cases be ordered to pay the costs. In my opinion, it is a fundamental and correct principle that the person to be judged should be ordered to pay the costs rather than the State. This applies regardless of whether the perpetrator is sentenced to an ordinary sentence or whether the perpetrator is free of punishment as a result of his condition and is instead sentenced to a treatment sentence. However, according to the rules in force, the courts may, after a specific assessment, decide that the liability for costs in criminal proceedings should be limited if it would otherwise be manifestly disproportionate to the guilt and conditions of the sentenced person. This exception applies today, for example, to cases where unnecessary expenditure has been incurred and in cases of principle which are being conducted by all the bodies to determine the future legal situation. In practice, however, the derogation is not applied in cases such as those we are discussing here today. I agree with the questioners that one might ask whether it is quite reasonable now that a convicted mentally deviant should in all cases be ordered to pay the costs. Therefore, in the next session of the Assembly, I will present a legislative proposal amending the exemption provided for in the Code of Civil Procedure, so that the courts will have a better opportunity to assess whether, in a specific trial, it is reasonable for a convicted person who is free of punishment as a result of his psychic condition to pay the costs of the proceedings. At the same time, the comments to the draft law will indicate that the current practice whereby a convicted person who is free of punishment as a result of his or her mental condition has to pay costs has to be changed, so that in future a concrete assessment of reasonableness can be carried out in which particular consideration of those persons can be taken into account. It will hardly be possible to draw up more firm guidelines, as these cases are very different and there is a great difference between how and to what extent a psychic deviation is expressed by the individual. That is why we need it to be assessed on a case-by-case basis in the courts. So the amendment of the law is simply intended to give the courts a better opportunity to balance the various concerns of these criminal proceedings. I am fully confident that the courts can, of course, deal with this within the framework that we are setting here in the Folketing. The courts already determine the penalty in criminal proceedings and are used to having to carry out concrete reasonable assessments. However, I hope that I have answered the question very clearly, and I am sure that we all share the view that society must of course conduct criminal proceedings against mentally deviant persons only if there is a specific need for it, and that is the way the rules are drawn up. Special attention is now being paid to mentally absenteeism and a possible treatment judgment will be determined according to what is necessary to prevent the person concerned from committing new crime. It's okay. It's okay. On the other hand, I agree with the questioners that it does not seem reasonable in all cases to have to pay the costs as well. And on that point there may be a need to change the rules, as I have then clarified. I hope that in this debate there will be well received the hand-out for a future amendment of the law. Thank you for the floor.

The Folketing acknowledges that it is a fundamental principle that the convicted person must pay the costs of the proceedings over the State in criminal proceedings. At the same time, the Folketing notes that the rules in force restrict the ability of the courts to make a concrete and individual assessment of the fairness of the payment of the costs of a convicted person who is free of punishment as a result of his mental condition. The Danish Parliament notes that, in the next Assembly, the Government intends to propose amendments to the Code of Procedure so that the courts can assess whether, in a specific criminal case, it is reasonable for a convicted person who is free of punishment as a result of his psychic condition to pay the costs of the proceedings. In criminal proceedings, the State bears the cost of the actual operation of the court, i.e. the judge's salary, the prosecutor's salary, the rent, etc., and if a defender is involved, the sentenced person must pay a fee for this, and that is surely the expense that we are talking about in the debate. Then how many cases are we talking about? I am not actually quite clear about this, but there has certainly been an increase after it became a condition that public servants, for example. Nursing staff must report a case to the police for compensation from the Counsel for Victims of Victims of Victims of Victims of Victims of Victims of Victims of Victims of Victims. I know that the Minister for Justice is looking at that rule, and there is good reason for this, since the police have enough to do, and since a sentence that is convicted is very rarely punished, according to the provisions of paragraphs 16 and 69 of the Criminal Code. Even if a dement is convicted but is exempted from punishment under these provisions, he is still in danger of being sentenced to legal costs. This may seem unreasonable, and we already have a provision in Article 1008 (1) of the Code of Procedure. 4, according to which the court may and I quote "limit the liability for costs where it considers that otherwise this would be manifestly disproportionate to the guilt and conditions of the sentenced person. It is obviously not being used, and we therefore welcome further clarification and the Minister's announced initiatives. ParlaMint-DK_20170420143815	Thank you. The question is whether people with dementia or mental illness, or who are otherwise permanently ill, should go through legal proceedings and pay legal costs. People with dementia, permanent inhabile or persons with other mental illness may find it difficult to understand and relate to what is happening around them, and therefore they may be at risk both to themselves and to others. We must help these people as best we can, but by violent behavior towards other people we must always take account of the victim. There must be a sense of justice and we must do what we can to prevent further attacks from taking place. There must therefore be procedures for deciding what to do with the violent person, even if he is a mentally ill person. Such a procedure may be criminal proceedings, which is far from always the case. However, for people who have been declared permanently ill due to dementia or other serious illness, there should be the possibility of an individual assessment, so as to avoid practices that are inappropriate, both for citizens and for society. Nowadays, there are already specific rules which require that in many cases where there are mentally deviant persons there should be indictment. In criminal proceedings where this is not done and the accused is judged, it is the convicted person himself who has to pay his costs, and not the State. That should continue to be the starting point. However, there may be cases in which it does not seem reasonable for a convicted person who is free of punishment as a result of his mental condition to pay the costs of the proceedings. I am therefore pleased that the Minister for Justice is going to look at a change of law that allows the courts to make an individual assessment of whether, in a specific case, it is reasonable for a convicted mental illness who is free of punishment to pay the costs of the proceedings. The Liberal Alliance supports the adoption text read out by Mrs Liselott Blixt.

Where in the past Danish police officers had to call an employee in Ejby in order to be able to look up a person in the SIS register, a Danish police officer must in future call an employee in The Hague in the Netherlands or possibly an seconded employee in Ejby, who can access the same information immediately and without having to ask permission. In practice, after 1 May, Danish officers will be fully present, as was the case before 1 May, and Denmark can also participate in the various task forces and working groups as before. This, as far as we have understood in the Danish People's Party, is not something you do quite often, and Denmark's voice has never been decisive in the outcome of a vote. ParlaMint-DK_20170420150910	Thank you. Back in December 2015, we were, as you know, in the ballot box for a referendum on the abolition of the Danish reservation of law. Based on that mandate, the government has had long and ambitious negotiations with the EU on the conclusion of an intergovernmental special agreement between Denmark and Europol. Thanks to the skillful work of our Prime Minister and our Minister for Justice, we have succeeded in achieving a special agreement with the closest possible link to European police cooperation, and the adoption of today's legislative proposal will certainly provide the basis for Denmark's continued association with Europol. My colleague and EU rapporteur for the Danish People's Party, Mr. Kenneth Kristensen Berth, has been so impressed by the Prime Minister's negotiating skills that he has referred in the media to the Danish special agreement with Europol as being as good as if Denmark had still been a full member. On behalf of the Prime Minister, I would like to thank you for that praise, although I think that reality looks just a little different. After all, it is overselling the agreement to say that it is as good as full membership of Europol would have been. The fact is that the agreement does not quite correspond to full membership. Among other things: Danish police are in a position to lose direct access to the registers, which are reserved exclusively for full members. Instead, the Danish police, as Mr. Kenneth Kristensen Berth explained, now request that searches be made in the databases. The National Police themselves point out that the disadvantages of the new system will increase as new and modern technology is applied. I have asked what exactly that means, and that means that when you, as a police officer, get a tablet at some point and thus have direct access to databases from the police car, that will not be possible. There are also major programmes in Europe currently under way in many countries on license plates and scanning of license plates, and here too it will be harder for Danish police when technology can be mastered. In addition, Denmark will only be able to participate in Europol's so-called Management Board as an observer, and this means in concrete terms that we cannot sit at the negotiating table and take decisions on Europol's development and priorities. So it may well be that some people do not think it is important, but I think it is really a pity for our police that we are cut off from these opportunities. Although the agreement has not yet been approved by the Council, the draft law is being first examined today, and this is due, of course, to the fact that, following the adoption of the bill, Danish legislation may be in place by 1 May 2017. The Government can thus accede to the agreement between Denmark and Europol as soon as the process of the EU institutions has been completed. So I very much hope that today Parliament will give its consent to the government's accession to an intergovernmental agreement on operational and strategic cooperation with Europol on behalf of Denmark. With the adoption of the agreement, we can continue to cooperate with our European neighbours and allies. We can ensure our police the best possible relationship to carry out their important work and stem cross-border crime from the situation in which the "no' in December 2015 put Denmark. Left votes in favour of the bill. I hope that all parties in the Folketing will do the same. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420151848	Today we are discussing the draft law on the conclusion of an intergovernmental agreement between Denmark and the European Union on Europol. In the Enhedslisten, we can agree to the bill. The agreement will enable Denmark, as in the past, to continue its operational cooperation with police authorities in other EU countries in the fight against cross-border crime, although on 1 May 2017 Europol will become supranational. The agreement, which has been called by some EU experts, quote, impressive good agreement, quote over, specifically means that Denmark will continue to have full access to all relevant information in the Europol criminal register. Today it is the case that investigators and patrol cars must call the National Police Office in Copenhagen if they want to search Europol's databases. Here they get a cop, and let's call the cop Jensen. Jensen is employed by the Danish police, and he conducts the search and passes on the results. With the new agreement, investigators and patrol cars will continue to be able to call the police station in Copenhagen and have a search conducted in Europol. The only difference will be that Officer Jensen, who is on the phone, is no longer employed by the Danish police, but instead is employed by Europol. From the point of view of the Ministry of Justice, we have had the new agreement well illustrated, as we got from the technical review, and the biggest difference between the two images is that one shows that there is a difference in colour between Mr. Jensen's tie, and that really is the only difference. Because a search is being carried out in exactly the same way as today, and we have exactly the same access to Europol's databases as we do today. We have noted in the Enhedslisten that Denmark will continue to have officers stationed at Europol headquarters in The Hague, as well as participation in Europol's Counter-Terrorism Centre and Cybercrime Centre. It is therefore very, very difficult to see that there should be anything at all that puts the Danish police operationally worse than what it is today, and that is why the National Police has also declared that the new agreement in a number of important areas is operationally satisfactory to the Danish police. Up to the referendum on 3 December, all logic was that a cooperation agreement could be drawn up between Denmark and Europol, even though the Danes voted no. In the Enhedslisten we pointed many times to the agreement which among other things. Norway has with Europol. Nevertheless, politicians and so-called experts on the 'yes' side were not late in pointing out all the accidents that would hit Denmark if the referendum were to end with a 'no'. Now it's a no. According to the Jaspers, before the referendum it had been quite unlikely that Denmark could get an agreement with Europol, and if it could possibly succeed, it was said that it would take 5 to 7 years. In the meantime, it could almost sound as if Denmark would become a mecca for paedophiles and criminals. Of course, it is always difficult to predict the future, but with the new Europol agreement I have to say frankly that the yes side has been taken well and thoroughly with its pants down. It has been revealed that the claim that it would be impossible to reach an agreement was merely a scaremongering campaign, a scaremongering campaign which should make voters vote 'yes' to abolishing the legal reservation. When we deal with the proposal today, I think it is important to mention that we in the Enhedslist are not completely uncritical of Europol. In the past, we have expressed scepticism as to whether Europol will have a number of powers under the new regulation, without legal certainty necessarily following suit. We have also seen the leak of data on suspects from Europol in the past. These are matters which we in the Enhedslist will, of course, follow very closely. It is therefore also important for us that this is an intergovernmental agreement with Europol. This means that Denmark can always withdraw from the agreement if we discover problems with the cooperation. In this way, we ensure that it is still the people who have the power to decide which rules should apply in Denmark.

Europol cooperation is of great importance for the work of the Danish police in preventing and combating cross-border crime. We all know that Denmark cannot combat serious international crime alone, and we all agree that the fight against cross-border crime requires close cooperation. This is why the government has worked hard and intensively to negotiate an agreement with the Commission and Europol. The agreement is not as good as membership of Europol, but the agreement will ensure that Denmark can maintain a link with Europol cooperation after 1 May 2017. The processes of the EU institutions will continue in this and in the coming week, and the agreement is not expected to be approved by the Council until Friday 28 April. The bill ensures that Danish legislation is in place before 1 May 2017, and also contains the consent of the Danish Parliament that the government can accede to the agreement between Denmark and Europol. Following the bill, it is proposed that the agreement between Denmark and Europol be implemented in Danish law by incorporation. The draft law thus implies that an agreement between Denmark and Europol will apply in Denmark following the announcement of the agreement in the Official Journal. When the proposal was tabled, the draft agreement was only available in English and work is currently being done to finalise the translation of the draft agreement into Danish. And I expect that the Danish text version of the draft agreement will be forwarded to the Danish Parliament at the beginning of the next week. ParlaMint-DK_20170420160743	The debate is open. President. - I call the Group of the European People's Party (ChristianDemocratic Group). Thomas Jensen as rapporteur for the Social Democratic Party. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420160804	Thank you, President. The draft law aims to make a number of adjustments to the rules on the recovery and resolution of credit institutions and investment firms. The rules are an implementation of the BRRD Directive, which is an abbreviation of The Bank Recovery and Resolution Directive, and the legislative proposal therefore makes changes and clarifications which ensure that the rules work as intended in the directive, while at the same time clarifying the division of roles between the authorities involved at home. There are a number of general points. The first point concerns group recovery plans and the draft law specifies which authorities are included in the joint decision on which authorities may make recommendations to the group recovery plan and it is specifically proposed that the FSA should submit both recovery plans and group recovery plans for Financial Stability A/S, which may make recommendations. That sounds quite reasonable and is something we can support. Then there are write-down liabilities, and here is the purpose of the rules to prevent companies from structure their obligations in a way that makes bail-in less effective. Bail-in is the principle that only those who have invested in the company should bear the losses. The current Danish translation of the directive is not in line with the English version of these provisions and, in order to ensure that Danish companies are not treated less favourably or differently than other companies in other countries, the provisions on eligible liabilities are proposed in accordance with the English version of the directive, so that the requirements on eligible liabilities can be set as a percentage of the capital base and total liabilities. That also sounds quite reasonable. Then there is the third point in the draft law which deals with the write-down and conversion of capital instruments. It is the case that it is clarified that the conditions under which an institution is no longer viable are the same as for assessing whether an institution is in need or is likely to be in need. And it also sounds quite reasonable to clarify that in the legislation. Then there is the fourth point about the fact that the Danish Financial Supervisory Authority must inform Financial Stability A/S when a company has to be wound up. And according to the current directive, before Financial Stability A/S initiates winding-up measures against an undertaking, the FSA must, after consulting Financial Stability A/S, assess that other options for managing the company have been exhausted. Financial Stability A/S then assesses whether the conditions for settlement are met under the law. However, the directive does not require the competent authority in Denmark, i.e. the FSA, to consult the resolution authority Financial Stability A/S, and to ensure a clear division of powers between supervisory and resolution authorities, to be adapted to the wording of the provision. That also sounds quite reasonable. Then there is the fifth point in the draft law, and this is about offsetting and reversing the restructuring and winding-up of certain financial undertakings. It is proposed that, in the context of restructuring and winding-up of a financial undertaking, Financial Stability A/S may establish claims in accordance with the same set-off principles as are applied in the event of bankruptcy. And by giving Financial Stability A/S that opportunity, it is even more ensured that it is primarily owners and creditors of the failing financial institution who bear the Institute's losses. I also think that this element is quite reasonable. So all in all, with these very technical elements, the Social Democrats think it is a sensible proposal that we can support.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420161156	Thank you to the Socialist rapporteur. Next in line, sir. Hans Kristian Skibby as rapporteur for the Danish People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420161204	The draft law we are discussing here is a legislative proposal aimed at implementing a number of minor changes in the financial field, such as: ensure that credit institutions and creditors are the first to bear the financial losses when the institution is in liquidation. The legislative changes contained in this bill also mean that a number of minor changes and clarifications are being made to the rules currently implemented through the EU directive on the recovery and winding-up of credit institutions and investment firms. In concrete terms, as the Social Democrat rapporteur also said, financial stability A/S, among other things. have access to take into account competitive principles of set-off and reversal in connection with the reconstruction and resolution of institutions. The legislative proposal also means that we are now being given the opportunity to ensure that any payments made by a company which has to close down can be made at a minimum and that those who bear the financial losses are mainly those who have also had the responsibility and the operation, etc. by the financial undertaking concerned. All in all, it is a legislative proposal that benefits Danish interests, and it is also a minimum implementation in relation to the EU directive. Against this background, the Danish People's Party can support the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420161333	Thank you to the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party. The next speaker is Mr. Torsten Schack Pedersen as rapporteur for the Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420161340	Thank you. At the risk of sounding a bit like an echo of the two previous rapporteurs, I would just like to mention some of the key points from this draft law: that it is the implementation of an EU directive, a series of clarifications and adjustments that ensure that the legislation is clear and that there is no doubt, nor that we are in a situation where the conditions that apply to Danish financial institutions are different from those in our neighbouring countries. Then there is also a provision that if you have not completed a course in board competence, which you must to sit on the board of a financial company, then it is now possible to grant an exemption if you have not completed it within 12 months, for example if you have become ill or have been on maternity leave, or that the course you are taking is not completed until the 12 months have passed. That kind of makes a lot of sense. It is important that the board members are well dressed, but a rigid date is even square enough. So if there is a need for a little extra rubber band to make sure that the board members get the necessary competencies, then it makes real sense. I would also like to point out that this proposal is important for the risk to be borne in the same way as in the event of bankruptcy if it is instead a restructuring that it is owners and creditors who have to bear the same loss as in the event of bankruptcy. In order to ensure that it is equal to bankruptcy in general, if it is through restructuring, one acts, yes, then there must also be the possibility of reverse transactions made up to the transfer or the situation where ownership changes as a result of an institution no longer being able to deal with the situation itself. If any provision has been made to give individual creditors a particularly favourable position, it should of course be no different from if the institution had gone bankrupt. Today, this can be reversed by bankruptcy, and it will also apply here. Basically, it is a question of ensuring that we comply with the common rules in this area in relation to the European Union, but also with clarity and clarification. It is therefore important that there are clear rules on financial matters. That is why it is, of course, a quite sensible legislative proposal which we in the Left support. I have promised to announce that so does the Conservative People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420161623	Thank you to the rapporteur of the Left. Next in line, sir. Pelle Dragsted as rapporteur for the Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420161637	Thank you, Mr President. After all, this is another of the many proposals we are dealing with in here that are about implementing EU regulations and directives into Danish legislation. One might sometimes ask how much political autonomy we actually have left here in Denmark. In this case, as I read this very complicated legislative proposal, there are some sensible initiatives to ensure that when banks and other financial companies are hit by a crisis or are in trouble, they can be settled with minimal damage to the economy. It is the investors who have to take the loss. They are also the ones who get the prize when things are going well and not the rest of us, society or the small savers. In fact, as I read the proposal, there are also some sensible tightenings in this area. Today, when a financial undertaking is wound up, Financial Stability must ensure that creditors do not incur a greater loss than would have been incurred if the entity or entity had been wound up. If they are suffering a greater loss, there must be compensation from the so-called winding-up formula of Financial Stability. As I understand the proposal, it may be the minister who can confirm it, the right, if it is to be said, to creditors will become less extensive in the future, because to some extent it is contrary to the thinking of this directive, namely the bail-in principle described by previous rapporteurs, that is to say the idea that it is investors who have to bear the loss. The changes will allow Financial Stability to tighten up in relation to these payments from the settlement formula. I think that makes a great deal of sense at the moment. So we are immediately in favour of this proposal. However, there is one concern. It concerns the right of the Minister of Industry to lay down detailed rules on winding-up, including that relating to the conditions of the customers taken over by the banks in question. The draft law states that the Minister may lay down detailed rules on the customer conditions of companies, including interest rates, fees, etc., as these are not to be attractive and do not have to compete with the conditions generally offered to customers of companies with similar business activities. It is quite far-reaching powers which can have quite far-reaching consequences for people's economy. With the history that we have and that we know from, for example. the customers of Roskilde Bank and many others, who were, after all, affected by some very, very severe interest rate increases in relation to Financial Stability, I can be a little concerned about this. ParlaMint-DK_20170420162315	Thank you to the radical rapporteur. I do not see the SF rapporteur in the Chamber, and that is why I am giving the floor to the Minister for Industry.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420162322	Thank you for the many, I think, constructive comments. The main aim of the draft law is to ensure that it is owners and creditors who bear all financial losses when a financial institution becomes in need and has to be wound up. This is entirely in line with the practice we have had in Denmark. At the same time, it is ensured that payments from the so-called winding-up formula, built up through contributions from all financial institutions, can be kept to an absolute minimum for the benefit of the sector as a whole. The draft law therefore gives Financial Stability access to e.g. to apply the principles of offsetting claims known under the Bankruptcy Act when Financial Stability is required to perform a restructuring or winding-up of a financial institution. The draft law also means that Danish financial institutions are not treated differently from companies in other EU countries. It is important that Danish financial companies have the opportunity to compete on equal terms with companies in the rest of the EU. The adjustments are made partly by a change in how the requirement for eligible liabilities is determined and partly by a change in the liabilities that can be used to meet the requirement. The purpose of the rules is to prevent the undertakings concerned from structuring their obligations in such a way as to make restructuring or winding-up more difficult or less effective. In addition, the draft law also clarifies the division of roles between the FSA and Financial Stability in the context of resolution planning, etc. Finally, the draft law provides for a derogation in relation to the conduct of a basic course by board members. The draft law thus allows the FSA to grant an exemption from the 12-month deadline for the completion of a basic course, provided that, for example, a member of the board of directors is entitled to grant an exemption from the 12 month deadline for the completion of a basic course. insufficient training provision, sickness or maternity has not been able to complete the course in good time. This avoids the risk that board members may be required to resign in situations where there is a legitimate explanation. Overall, the proposal aims to ensure that the financial sector is well regulated, sound and robust, while at the same time there is fairness in regulation. I look forward to the discussion of the draft law in committee and, of course, I would be happy to ask if there are further questions in the committee's proceedings.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420162759	The debate is open. I give the floor to Mr Seligman to speak on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian-Democratic Group) in order to speak on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian-Democratic
ParlaMint-DK_20170420162821	Thank you, President. The Danish mortgage is one of the world's cheapest forms of housing financing. It is this form of financing that ensures that many families who do not have millions of kroner on the bank book can nevertheless realise the dream of a house and that the entrepreneurs can realise a good framework for their businesses. In other words, the mortgage system helps to support economic activity in Denmark by providing cheap financing for both households and businesses. From a political point of view, it is important that we keep an eye on the proper functioning of the mortgage credit system and that consumers experience a market characterised by relevant information and thus transparency. And if you, as a borrower, want to move your loan to another institute, there must also be a real opportunity to do so. In spring 2016, we saw how a number of mortgage banks announced significant increases in the contribution rates on mortgage loans. This gave rise to a particularly welcome debate on how institutions and banks should act if borrowers suddenly have to pay more for their loans. Since then, a committee of experts has looked at ways of strengthening transparency and mobility in the mortgage market. The Committee of Experts has made a number of sensible recommendations for necessary adjustments to the rules. And this bill we are discussing today is then the result of the political agreement on increased transparency and mobility in the mortgage market, which we concluded in January, based on the recommendations of the experts. The content of the draft law is to ensure a longer warning period. It is the case that as a consumer, as a borrower, from the day when an increase in contributions is announced, not now only has three months, but six months to assess whether one should continue to have one's loan in that institution or whether one might move to another institute. Then something new is being introduced: a justification for these increases. Overall, the reasons for possible price increases by institutions must be more transparent for borrowers. The institution's warning of the changes in contributions, interest rate supplements and charges shall be justified by the conditions that have triggered the increase. In addition, institutions shall explain the main factors having an impact on the extent of the change, as well as their estimated proportion of the overall increase. These must not be just justifications; they must also be adequate justifications. In fact, increases in contributions, interest rates and charges may only be implemented if they are duly justified. If there is no notice of increases in contributions and increases in interest rates according to the rules, they cannot enter into force. There must also be more and better information when you are notified of this increase. Institutions and banks shall provide the borrower with more information when alerting price increases. The additional information shall include, inter alia: information on the market value of the customer's loans. Institutions and banks shall also provide the borrower with information that the borrower has the possibility to repay the loan and to convert it to another loan, where appropriate, at the same institution or at another loan provider. There must also be information on where to go and compare what the various lenders offer. Then it is also the case that the borrower's costs must be reduced. It shall not be permitted to charge redemption fees during the warning period by an increase in the contribution rate of a mortgage institution and by an increase in the interest rate supplement for mortgage-like loans in a financial institution. Furthermore, no more than half of the sum of curfews and price cuts should be charged for the repayment of loans during the warning period. Then, for the second time, we remove the tape here in Denmark. Indeed, it is the case that the legislation does not today allow the borrower to transfer his entire mortgage loan to another mortgage institution if the loan limit is exceeded and that limits the mobility of the mortgage market. This can be removed, we think, since a change in legislation will not increase the overall risk to the mortgage sector. Furthermore, the borrowers, both private and corporate customers, must be able to move to another institution by conversion or loan conversion, even if the loan exceeds the loan limit. Transparency must be increased, and here we come to some of the things that are agreed in the political agreement to increase: transparency in terms of price cut and greater transparency in relation to the distribution of costs in loan offers. It is introduced by amendments to the Order of Good Practice for Housing Credit. Once a year, the Danish Competition and Consumer Protection Agency, in cooperation with the Consumer Ombudsman, will have to explain the development of contributions rates, interest rate supplements, etc. and in the market conditions in which institutions operate. In conclusion, it is the Social Democrats' expectation that the measures outlined will increase transparency and mobility in the mortgage market. We can therefore support the bill. However, I would also add that it is our clear expectation in the Social Democratic Party that institutions will have the opportunity to lower contributions rates and interest rate supplements once they have built up sufficient capital and reserves in relation to adopted and expected new capital requirements.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420164344	The history of the bill here has been affected, but allow me to repeat that it is well over a year since a large number of homeowners in Denmark experienced that their contribution rates increased significantly. This caused great frustration, for many customers felt that they were very locked to the institute they were in and that the possibilities of moving their loans and making use of the competition were not enough present. At that time, the Minister for Business and Growth Troels Lund Poulsen set up a committee of experts to look at the transparency and competition in the mortgage market; a committee of experts then delivered its seven recommendations in September last year. And on the basis of those recommendations and a political debate over a certain period of time, we then arrived at a very broad political agreement in January of this year. It is an agreement that basically aims to strengthen transparency and strengthen competition in the Danish mortgage market. For this is the best way to ensure that consumers benefit from free competition • that it is easier to change mortgage institutions and that there is much more transparency in prices and conditions so that consumers can use their right to trade elsewhere. In concrete terms, and I know that a number of the elements have already been mentioned, it is fundamental that the warning period should be extended from 3 to 6 months and that a much better explanation should be given when increasing contributions are announced. If the explanations are not good enough, the possibility of that lapses. And then, as has also been mentioned, we remove the possibility of charging a redemption fee. We have then come to the conclusion that only half the curfew and price cut may be charged. And, as the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party said, it was an issue on which opinions were divided. It was not, after all, a wish that we did not want to make it as cheap as possible for customers, but on the basis that it was in fact one of the elements in which we saw that there was free competition. In fact, it was one of the elements on which competition took place, on top of the rising rates of contributions that one institute, in particular, came up with last year. There were competing institutes that just struck on this as a parameter for why you had to switch to their institute, and there they significantly reduced the price. I think there were some who even completely removed it. However, we then landed on a plane to make sure that there is broad political support that a maximum of half can be charged in curtage and price cut. I think it is important that there is broad political support in this area. I will come back to that. I would also like to highlight the element which actually goes beyond the recommendations and on which there was disagreement in the Committee of Experts, namely that it will be possible to have the possibility, if another mortgage credit institution so wishes, of taking over a loan from a borrower, even if the loan limit has been exceeded. I also noted, as the rapporteur for the Danish People's Party did, that the National Bank was not enthusiastic about it. It may be at a sharper price. At any rate, the situation which I have unfortunately been presented with many examples of the fact that if your loan limit has been exceeded, then there is, in fact, relatively much room for a mortgage credit institution to raise the contribution rates, because as a customer you do not have the opportunity to move. I'm really glad we brought that element. In fact, I think that will have a major impact on competition for those concerned. In general, the consultation responses to the proposal are also quite neat. There are some small details which I am quite sure we can finally uncover during the committee work. There's a lot of credibility. Our mortgage bonds are internationally rated as bottom-solid. And then I think it is important that we also have a political role to play in ensuring a high level of trust and transparency and proper competition, so that we are sure that the Danish home owners will get their mortgages at the highest possible price. That is why, of course, we support the draft law. And I have promised to announce that so does the Conservative People's Party.
