ParlaMint-DK_20170420165234	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then the next rapporteur is Mr. Pelle Dragsted, and it's still Enhedslisten. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420165247	Thank you. The security of our housing economy is perhaps one of the most central economic issues for most families, whether you own your home, whether you rent your home or whether you are in work or are looking for work. Sudden changes in our economic conditions, whether they come from rising tax payments or from rising contribution rates or from rising rents, are something that creates enormous insecurity for Danish families. And that is why we have also been very concerned and active in the Enhedslisten about the problem we have seen in recent years, namely that, yes, hundreds of thousands of Danish homeowners have experienced exploding contributions rates, that is, contributions rates that could increase by many, many thousands of kroner. This is a serious problem and it has been taken far too little seriously by political groups. The fact is that in the old days we had mortgage associations, which functioned on the basis of a self-sustaining principle. The contribution rates had to cover the costs, and they had to collect, you might say, any losses. A shared responsibility for this was taken. That's what the contribution rates were supposed to cover. This is no longer the case because most mortgage providers have now become part of large financial groups. They are owned by the banks and they must be paid, and we must then promise that that is the case. After all, we have seen how too completely open a blanket has stuck its fingers in the pockets of Danish home owners on the pretext that it is a matter of padding, raising the contribution rates, and so it has been channeled right over to the banks and their shareholders. One of the reasons why you have been able to do this is, of course, the injustice that is that in mortgage loans there is the built-in possibility that you can constantly change the terms, primarily in terms of how much is paid in contributions. This can be said to be okay in the old days, because nobody had to profit from it, so there was an expectation that if the contribution rates increased, then it was because it was needed to cover any collective losses or because there was growing administrative costs. But that is not the case today. Instead, it has become such a Trojan horse into the economy of the homeowners. To make things worse, we also have a situation where it is difficult for customers to vote with their feet and choose another mortgage institution. There are some positive elements in this proposal, but in our view it is far from far enough. For example: It is a good thing that in future we will have to justify the increase in the rate of contributions, but there are no specific requirements as to the justifications to be given. There may be many reasons that can be used which do not seem reasonable, but as long as they are disclosed, that is the way it is. It is also difficult to assess whether these reasons are the real reasons. After all, we have seen that there have been completely incorrect reasons. It has been said that it was about padding, but in reality it was about something quite different, namely channelling money to its shareholders. It is also good that, in future, the costs of changing institutions will be reduced after the rates of contributions have been increased. We would have liked the Consumer Council to have followed the line of thinking by going even harder in terms of redemption fees and in relation to curfew and price cut, but even if you had chosen that model, the problem that I also mentioned before was still faced, namely that there may be other significant costs involved in moving your loan, for example. if the interest rate has risen in the meantime. So the reality is that in this area we will never establish a real competitive situation, as in other areas we can do, or what we could imagine, because there will always be some who have a real difficulty in moving. And that, unfortunately, gives the banks and the bank-owned mortgage banks too much opportunity to squeeze the lemon. So we need a much harder regulation of this area. Basically, we need to go for a regulation that says that it is fine to increase the contribution rates if there is a proper justification for this. And that reason must be approved by supervision. It was not necessary at the time the mortgage banks were, as they were a few years ago, when they had a rest in themselves. But it is necessary today if we are to protect Danish homeowners from becoming economic dairy cattle for the banks, which are behind most mortgage institutions today. So we support this proposal on the principle that it is an improvement, but unfortunately it does not solve the problem. It does not restore any security whatsoever for the homeowners, and it will not be the last time we will be discussing this issue here in the Folketing.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420165808	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mr Beazley. Joachim B. Olsen, Liberal Alliance. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420165820	Thank you for that, President. This bill is the implementation of the agreement on increased transparency and mobility in the mortgage market, which was concluded earlier this year by a broad section of the Folketing. The agreement is again a consequence of the debate that arose in the wake of Nykredit's, Totalkredit's increase in their contribution rates in 2016. I said then, and I still think it was a very unsightly debate we had. A private company in competition with other private companies must be able to raise prices without political intervention. But we have now reached this agreement and it contains various elements, some of which are sensible enough, but I also think I would be lying if I said that we would have concluded the agreement if we had not been in government. It would probably be quite easy to demonstrate that we did not, because when we had the debate, we were not in government, and there I and LA criticised the debate and the desire for intervention on its basis. But now there are some changes, and that is one of them. changes which give rise to information on the reasons for the increase in contributions, even very specific information on the percentage basis of the changes in contributions. I think that may be a little strange, because it is difficult for a mortgage credit institution, just as it is for all other companies, to say crown-to- krone why you raise the price of the product. If we now imagined that we should ask Arla or others for crowns to say why they raised the price of a litre of milk by 25 cents, they would probably have difficulty with it, as it is quite complicated, but nevertheless we are now asking mortgage banks to do so. Then the warning period is increased from 3 to 6 months. It will be prohibited to charge redemption fees and, in future, an institution will not be able to charge more than half of the sum at the time of price cut when loans are settled during the early warning period, by increasing the contribution rate of a mortgage credit institution and by increasing the interest rate supplement on mortgage-like loans in financial institutions. I think there is one thing that should be taken into account, whether you think this is a very good proposal or whether you think it is a less good proposal, and that is that the costs that are running a mortgage institution and also know that people change mortgage institutions and repay loans etc. do not disappear. They will then be imposed on other homeowners. One can then discuss how reasonable it is that the costs that are, by acting and choosing to act also as a customer, must be borne by those customers who do not choose to act. I do not think it is particularly reasonable and I think it would be fair for the Folketing to be honest that the costs are now being applied to other homeowners. I do not think this has been very clear in the debate, but I think it is fair to say so. But we are in the agreement, which is why, of course, we stand by it, and that is why we naturally support the bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420172847	Thank you for your comments and for the good dialogue that I think there has been. This is, of course, an extremely important topic for a great many Danes. It is about the most crucial thing in the private economy of most families, namely the purchase of own housing where you need mortgage loans. It is about ensuring the greatest possible transparency and transparency, as much competition as possible and as cheaply as possible. It is also part of the basis for a broad political agreement. Then there are some who have a little more ambition, but everyone agrees that some sensible steps have been taken. We reached this agreement earlier this year with the clear aim of increasing transparency and mobility in the mortgage market. The mortgage credit system plays a very important role in the overall supply of finance in Denmark and thus in economic activity in this country. The proper functioning of the mortgage credit system is therefore of great importance. The draft law provides for a rapid follow-up to the Committee's report and provides a basis for increased transparency and mobility in the mortgage market. It must be easier for borrowers to understand what opportunities there are for changing institutions if price increases are announced, and it must also be cheaper for borrowers to move institutions. We do this by ensuring that the borrower gets information about what is at the root of a price increase. Similarly, the borrower must be informed, in connection with the warning of price increases, of, inter alia: opportunities to repay the loan. In addition, we extend the warning period and provide a framework for the costs that may be incurred by borrowers in changing institutions. We are introducing, among other things, a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to biological agents at work. the prohibition on the collection of redemption fees and institutions are only able to collect half of the sum resulting from curfew and price cut during the warning period. In addition, we shall ensure that there is clarity about the impact on borrowers if institutions do not justify an announced increase in contributions properly or do not comply with the six-month warning period. In such cases, increases in contributions and charges could not be charged to the borrowers. If the rules are not complied with, the institution shall start afresh and notify price increases anew. In order to increase mobility in the mortgage market, we also open the way for borrowers to move to another mortgage institution, even if the mortgage loan exceeds the loan limits. Overall, I am pleased that we have achieved the broad political agreement. Indeed, it is good initiatives that improve the mortgage credit system and I look forward to the committee's examination of the legislative proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170420174726	Thank you, sir. President. I will not hide that at all this evening as a model in relation to our future relationship with the European Union, which we would like the Danes to have the opportunity to take a position on when we have a clarification of the British situation. But we very much hope that the government will come a little out of the sand with what negotiating position the government intends to move towards. ParlaMint-DK_20170420175008	That is exactly what we are about to do now, namely the response from the Foreign Minister. So he has the floor now. Welcome.
ParlaMint-DK_20170420175018	Thank you. First of all, let me make it clear that the government attaches great importance to informing and involving the Danish Parliament in the preparations for Brexit. Questions have been answered in the Foreign Policy Name, just as both the Name and the European Committee have had the opportunity to discuss the issue in connection with presentations and reports from the summits. In addition, I and my predecessor have hosted informal breakfast meetings on Brexit with the participation of the European Committee and the Board of Governors. I hope that we can hold similar events forward-looking. Finally, we will discuss Brexit with the European Committee when the matter is on the agenda for Council meetings and summits. When it comes to the precise negotiating position in relation to the forthcoming complex and comprehensive negotiations on British withdrawal, the questioners will be aware that tomorrow the government will be presenting the matter, as was also mentioned, to the European Parliament's Committee on European Affairs in camera. This is not an expression of unnecessary secrecy, but for good reasons the government does not wish to publish the Danish negotiating position, since it would weaken the ability to best protect the interests of Danish citizens and companies if Britain had a detailed insight into what we are thinking here at the threshold of negotiations. However, there is no doubt that the British withdrawal from the EU will have a major impact on both Britain and the other EU countries. In relation to Brexit, of course, I would like to share the general considerations of the government, which are the basis of the government's proposals for the Danish negotiating position. The government respects Britain's decision to leave the EU and the British Government's desire for a clear break with the EU and the internal market. I do not want to hide the fact that we would have preferred Britain to remain part of the internal market, but we must now try to get the best possible result out of the forthcoming negotiations and we will work to ensure that both sides, both sides, it is important for the government to state in this context that the situation we find ourselves in has not arisen because Britain has been pressured to leave the internal market because of freedom of movement; Britain itself has decided that it no longer wants to be part of the EU's common regulatory framework and of the European Court of Justice, which is the foundation of the internal market. This is clear from their letter of notification to the European Union. It is precisely these common rules that are effectively enforced by national authorities, by courts, by the Commission and, ultimately, by the European Court of Justice that allow citizens and businesses to trade freely across borders. It has been Britain's very own political choice. This does not mean that, after they have resigned, Britain cannot gain access to the internal market in line with other third countries. The specific framework for this will have to be negotiated as part of a free trade agreement, and I will then come back to that. One of the first major issues to be agreed between the United Kingdom and the other EU countries is the precise organisation of the negotiations. On the British side, the wish has been expressed that negotiations on settlements and the future relationship should be opened in parallel. However, there is expected to be a broad consensus within the EU-27 circle that it is first necessary to clarify the general principles of the major settlement issues resulting from the divorce, including, among other things, the following: the issues of the financial balance and the rights of EU citizens in Britain and vice versa. The reasons why the EU-27 wants to start with the general principles of the major settlement issues resulting from the divorce are as follows: firstly, Brexit will include a number of important and complex issues for both citizens and companies, which need to be clarified for reasons of legal certainty before any meaningful discussion on future matters can be made. Secondly, failure to clarify the financial balance of the settlement could lead to a considerable additional cost for the remaining EU countries, for example. Britain has undertaken a number of financial commitments as an EU member, which will run for a number of years. If the British side refuses to live up to them, it could result in a considerable extra bill for Denmark and thus for the Danes. Thirdly, clarification on the key issues of settlements could create a good negotiating climate so that negotiations can be extended relatively quickly to cover the future, including the architecture of a trade agreement. In the worst case scenario, at the end of the two-year period, there is no risk of agreement on anything, and in every way it will be both expensive and difficult. That is why the EU-27 attaches great importance to the organisation of the negotiations. This also applies to those countries which attach particular importance to continued very close cooperation with Britain in the future. Ideally, I hope that we can reach agreement relatively quickly on the general principles for dealing with the major settlement issues, and when that is in place, the negotiations can then be extended to cover the future relationship, and then the negotiations should be able to take place in parallel. During the spring we have been given more clarity about the British wishes with Prime Minister May's speech in January, the British Government's White Paper on the approach to the forthcoming negotiations and the actual notification, where Britain does not want to be part of the EU's common regulatory framework, will not be included in the European Court of Justice, does not want to be included in the free movement and will not make a general contribution to the EU's budget. At the same time, the British state that independent free trade agreements will be concluded with third countries. Against this background, the British have decided to leave the internal market. Instead, a comprehensive free trade agreement with the EU will be sought, coupled with agreements in areas such as internal and external security, foreign policy, security policy, research, technology, innovation, etc. However, more precise details remain as to how the British want to be closely associated with the European Union. An agreement on Britain's withdrawal and future cooperation with the EU must be based on the right balance between rights and obligations. On the Danish side, we have a clear focus on securing our interests, not least for the companies and their employees. We cannot have a situation where British companies have full and free access to the EU's internal market and Danish companies face unequal conditions of competition, technical barriers to trade, state aid, etc. in the UK market, so that unfair competitive advantages arise for UK companies compared to Danish companies. Such a development could undermine the internal market and relations with third countries, in which we do not have an interest. Instead, we will work to maintain a level playing field so that we can lay the foundations for economic growth both in Britain and in the European Union. The government's first priority in the forthcoming negotiations will be to promote Danish interests, not least for companies and citizens. The work of the government's Brexit task force has from day to day focused on both issues related to the settlement division and the forward-looking relationship. In relation to the settlement division, I would like to highlight two points in particular. The first is that there is a good and fair solution, so that Britain will bear the costs that have been incurred by the British side, otherwise it can be very expensive for Danish taxpayers. The second is that, within the framework of the EU's negotiations with Britain, we ensure clear and fair solutions from the large number of Danish citizens living in Britain, and vice versa. Although this is an issue on which perhaps political agreement can be reached quickly, it should not be underestimated how difficult it can be to ensure effective solutions for a very complex area that can work in practice, especially when Britain does not want to be subject to EU rules. In relation to the forward-looking relationship, Danish interests are also at stake, not least in relation to market access. The United Kingdom is one of our five largest export markets with a broad composition ranging from agricultural products to industrial products to services. Like other EU countries, Danish fishermen fish in British waters and a good solution will also have to be found. There are many Danish jobs at stake here. We therefore have a general interest in maintaining maximum access to the British market, but it will hardly be simple with the British decision to leave the internal market. As has already been said, we must be aware that an agreement does not lead to Danish companies experiencing unequal competition from UK competitors in the internal market in the future. It is therefore a clear Danish interest to ensure a balance between rights and obligations in an agreement on the future relationship. The EU-27 is ready to start negotiations, and the December Summit Declaration and the draft guidelines outline the overall framework for the EU's approach to the negotiations. The Commission will conduct the forthcoming negotiations on the part of the EU-27, with the ongoing involvement of the Member States, and the European Parliament will, as you know, have to approve the final agreement. As you know, an extraordinary summit has been convened on 29 April, when the Heads of State or Government of the EU-27 will adopt the guidelines. Against this background, the Commission will present a proposal for a more detailed negotiating mandate which will have to be approved by the Council. After that, negotiations with the United Kingdom will begin. We are pleased by the government that we will soon be able to get started. We have waited a long time for the notification by the United Kingdom and we should not help delay the negotiations. Clarity is needed and that our citizens and our businesses have something to look to and look forward to where there is clarity. Overall, the Danish negotiating position will be based on the desire for continued close and good cooperation with the UK and the EU, and from the Danish side we will work to ensure that the negotiations get under way by a relatively rapid clarification of the principles of the major settlement issues, such as: the financial balance and citizens' rights in order to create the best framework in which to negotiate the future relationship in parallel. During the negotiations, we from Denmark will focus on ensuring Danish interests for our citizens and companies. A future agreement with the United Kingdom must, of course, balance rights and obligations, among others. The integrity of the internal market and equal conditions of competition between Great Britain and the EU will be central. It is the government's view that this is the way to safeguard Danish interests, including the many jobs at stake.
